I don't really know if I'd call it worrying, exactly. I mean, I haven't really noticed myself stressing over every little thing outside of the office. Tired? I don't know, not really. I mean, I'm not falling asleep at my desk or anything. I just sort of exist through the day, drink the breakroom coffee, and keep staring at my screens. 

Though, honestly, they just replaced the old coffee maker with this massive, overly complicated Italian machine that takes up half the counter. It's so loud. The facilities manager, Greg, ordered it, and he's always buying these flashy things to make it look like our office culture is so vibrant and energetic. It's just exhausting to look at. Like last month, he bought these bright neon beanbag chairs for the "collaboration space." Who is collaborating on a beanbag? We're financial analysts, not a tech startup in 2012. It just feels so forced and hollow. I had to sit in one once when the regular chairs were taken, and it took me forever to get out of it. My skirt got completely wrinkled, and I had to go into a meeting with the VP looking like an absolute mess. I don't know why Greg still has a job, to be honest. 

But... um, what were we talking about? Oh, fatigue. Yeah, no, I don't really notice being excessively tired during the day. I just keep going.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Physically restless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really fidget or pace around or anything. I actually sit pretty still. 

My sister did tell me I should try going to one of those sound baths to "relax" my body, though, so I went to this place downtown last weekend. It was ridiculously overpriced, first of all. They charged seventy dollars for forty-five minutes of some guy hitting brass bowls with a mallet. And the bowls weren't even polished. I mean, if you're going to charge a premium rate, your equipment should reflect that, right? It's just basic business sense. 

It actually reminded me of when I have to look at the expense reports for these mid-cap tech firms and they have all these phantom write-offs for "wellness retreats" that are really just luxury vacations in Tulum. I saw one last year where they wrote off a four-thousand-dollar dinner as team building. Four thousand dollars! The IRS would have an absolute field day if they actually looked at those itemized receipts. I mean, how many margaritas can a team of six even drink? It's blatant fraud, but nobody seems to care anymore. Everyone just rubber-stamps everything. 

But... sorry, what was the question? Oh, being restless. No, I don't really have that issue. I usually just sit on the couch.

Well, I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I'll just lie awake replaying client models in my head for hours. And then I'll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach completely tight, and I just end up going to work feeling like I never actually rested at all. 

It's actually really frustrating because I bought this new mattress last month. It was supposed to be one of those hybrid ones with the cooling gel layer that all the reviews said would change your life. I spent three thousand dollars on it, and the delivery guys scuffed my hardwood floor in the hallway when they brought it in. I tried calling the company's customer service to complain about the damage, but I just got stuck in this endless phone tree. "Press 1 for sales, press 2 for returns." I swear, companies don't want you to actually talk to a human being anymore. They just want you to give up. 

It's like this one telecom company we were analyzing last quarter. They completely restructured their customer support division to rely almost entirely on AI chatbots, and their stock went up 12% just on the announcement. But their actual customer retention numbers are terrible. Nobody looks at the long-term fundamentals anymore, they just look at the short-term cost savings. It's so short-sighted. I tried pointing this out in the committee meeting, but... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, falling asleep. Yeah, it takes hours, and then I'm up at 3 AM. It's just not working.

Unrefreshed? Yeah, I mean, like I said, I feel like I never actually rested. Even if I get a couple of hours, I wake up feeling completely hollowed out. It's like my brain is still running the numbers while my body is just... there. 

It reminds me of my car battery, actually. I have this German sedan, and the battery is supposed to last for five years, but it died after three. I took it to the dealership, and they tried to tell me it was because I don't drive it enough on the highway to charge the alternator. I drive twenty miles each way to the office! But they wouldn't honor the warranty. The service manager, this guy named Chad with way too much hair gel, just kept pointing at the fine print on his little tablet. It's ridiculous how these luxury brands treat you once they have your money. 

And don't even get me started on the waiting room there. They had this espresso machine—not like the one at work, but still annoying—and the TV was blasting some morning talk show where they were discussing "superfoods." They spent ten minutes talking about chia seeds. Chia seeds! They're just seeds! People will buy anything if you put the word "super" in front of it. We saw a whole portfolio of these health-food startups tank last year because their entire business model was based on Instagram influencers pretending to like kale powder. It's all just marketing smoke and mirrors. 

But... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, waking up unrefreshed. Yeah, definitely. I never feel rested.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, staying awake isn't really the issue, but concentrating... yeah. It's getting bad. Spreadsheets that used to be second nature to me now take hours. I just... I lose the thread. I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over, and I still worry I've missed something obvious before a presentation. 

It's actually a lot like what happened with my dry cleaner last week. I've been going to the same place for six years, right? And I dropped off this silk blouse—the navy one I usually wear for quarterly reviews. When I went to pick it up, they had completely lost it. The owner, Mr. Kim, spent twenty minutes looking through the racks, and then he tried to tell me I never brought it in. I showed him the ticket! It had the itemized number right there. But their new computer system—which looks like it was built in 1998, by the way—didn't have it logged properly. 

It's exactly the kind of operational inefficiency we look for when we're doing a teardown of a struggling retail chain. You see these legacy systems, and the management refuses to upgrade because of the upfront capital expenditure, but they bleed money in lost inventory and customer churn. I was looking at a regional department store's Q3 numbers just last month, and their shrinkage was up 4% year-over-year entirely because of outdated point-of-sale software. It's just... it's basic infrastructure. If you can't track a navy silk blouse, how are you tracking your quarterly revenue? 

But anyway... sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus on the numbers anymore. It takes me forever to get through a simple model.

My aunt had this beautiful estate, by the way, filled with all these antique clocks that would chime at different times. It drove me crazy when we were clearing it out. \n\nBut, um... what were you asking me? Oh, right, worrying. No, I don't really think I have a problem with that. It's just the work spreadsheets that get stuck in my head when I'm trying to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day because of your poor sleep at night?\n\nPatient: Tired? We're financial analysts, not a tech startup in 2012. It just feels so forced and hollow. I had to sit in one once when the regular chairs were taken, and it took me forever to get out of it. My skirt got completely wrinkled, and I had to go into a meeting with the VP looking like an absolute mess. I don't know why Greg still has a job, to be honest. \n\nBut... um, what were we talking about? Oh, fatigue. Yeah, no, I don't really notice being excessively tired during the day. I don't really fidget or pace around or anything. I actually sit pretty still. \n\nMy sister did tell me I should try going to one of those sound baths to \"relax\" my body, though, so I went to this place downtown last weekend. It was ridiculously overpriced, first of all. They charged seventy dollars for forty-five minutes of some guy hitting brass bowls with a mallet. And the bowls weren't even polished. I mean, if you're going to charge a premium rate, your equipment should reflect that, right? It's just basic business sense. \n\nIt actually reminded me of when I have to look at the expense reports for these mid-cap tech firms and they have all these phantom write-offs for \"wellness retreats\" that are really just luxury vacations in Tulum. I saw one last year where they wrote off a four-thousand-dollar dinner as team building. Four thousand dollars! The IRS would have an absolute field day if they actually looked at those itemized receipts. I mean, how many margaritas can a team of six even drink? It's blatant fraud, but nobody seems to care anymore. Everyone just rubber-stamps everything. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, being restless. No, I don't really have that issue. I usually just sit on the couch.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long it usually takes you to fall asleep, and how often you wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I'll just lie awake replaying client models in my head for hours. And then I'll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach completely tight, and I just end up going to work feeling like I never actually rested at all. \n\nIt's actually really frustrating because I bought this new mattress last month. It was supposed to be one of those hybrid ones with the cooling gel layer that all the reviews said would change your life. I spent three thousand dollars on it, and the delivery guys scuffed my hardwood floor in the hallway when they brought it in. I tried calling the company's customer service to complain about the damage, but I just got stuck in this endless phone tree. \"Press 1 for sales, press 2 for returns.\" I swear, companies don't want you to actually talk to a human being anymore. They just want you to give up. \n\nIt's like this one telecom company we were analyzing last quarter. Irritable? Well, I don't know if it's the sleep, but yeah, I guess I'm pretty on edge lately. Everything just feels like it's grating on me. 

Like the other day at the grocery store. I just wanted to get some of those honeycrisp apples, right? I asked him nicely if I could squeeze by, and he gave me this look, like I was the one inconveniencing him. It's just... the lack of basic customer service everywhere is astounding. 

It's a huge problem in the service sector right now. We were looking at labor productivity metrics for a major supermarket chain, and their turnover rate is so high they can't even maintain basic store standards. They're spending millions on these automated checkout kiosks to reduce headcount, but the machines are always breaking down. You go in, and there's a line of fifteen people waiting for one poor teenager to come clear an "unexpected item in bagging area" error. It's a complete misallocation of resources. If they just paid a competitive wage and trained their staff, their throughput would be so much better. I mean, the margins in grocery are razor-thin to begin with, you can't afford that kind of friction at the point of sale. I was trying to explain this to the junior analyst on my team, but he just kept staring at me like...

I don't really fidget or pace around or anything. I actually sit pretty still. \n\nMy sister did tell me I should try going to one of those sound baths to \"relax\" my body, though, so I went to this place downtown last weekend. It was ridiculously overpriced, first of all. They charged seventy dollars for forty-five minutes of some guy hitting brass bowls with a mallet. And the bowls weren't even polished. I mean, if you're going to charge a premium rate, your equipment should reflect that, right? It's just basic business sense. \n\nIt actually reminded me of when I have to look at the expense reports for these mid-cap tech firms and they have all these phantom write-offs for \"wellness retreats\" that are really just luxury vacations in Tulum. I saw one last year where they wrote off a four-thousand-dollar dinner as team building. Four thousand dollars! The IRS would have an absolute field day if they actually looked at those itemized receipts. I mean, how many margaritas can a team of six even drink? It's blatant fraud, but nobody seems to care anymore. Everyone just rubber-stamps everything. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, being restless. No, I don't really have that issue. I usually just sit on the couch.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long it usually takes you to fall asleep, and how often you wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I'll just lie awake replaying client models in my head for hours. And then I'll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach completely tight, and I just end up going to work feeling like I never actually rested at all. \n\nIt's actually really frustrating because I bought this new mattress last month. It was supposed to be one of those hybrid ones with the cooling gel layer that all the reviews said would change your life. I spent three thousand dollars on it, and the delivery guys scuffed my hardwood floor in the hallway when they brought it in. I tried calling the company's customer service to complain about the damage, but I just got stuck in this endless phone tree. \"Press 1 for sales, press 2 for returns.\" I swear, companies don't want you to actually talk to a human being anymore. They just want you to give up. \n\nIt's like this one telecom company we were analyzing last quarter. They completely restructured their customer support division to rely almost entirely on AI chatbots, and their stock went up 12% just on the announcement. I have this German sedan, and the battery is supposed to last for five years, but it died after three. Chia seeds! They're just seeds! People will buy anything if you put the word \"super\" in front of it. I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble staying awake or find it hard to concentrate during the day because of your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Well, staying awake isn't really the issue, but concentrating... yeah. But their new computer system—which looks like it was built in 1998, by the way—didn't have it logged properly. \n\nIt's exactly the kind of operational inefficiency we look for when we're doing a teardown of a struggling retail chain. I was looking at a regional department store's Q3 numbers just last month, and their shrinkage was up 4% year-over-year entirely because of outdated point-of-sale software. It's just... it's basic infrastructure. If you can't track a navy silk blouse, how are you tracking your quarterly revenue? \n\nBut anyway... sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, concentrating. It's just... the lack of basic customer service everywhere is astounding. \n\nIt's a huge problem in the service sector right now. You go in, and there's a line of fifteen people waiting for one poor teenager to come clear an \"unexpected item in bagging area\" error. It's a complete misallocation of resources. If they just paid a competitive wage and trained their staff, their throughput would be so much better. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, it's definitely affecting work. I mean, I actually got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations just keep slipping. I keep missing things. 

It's so embarrassing, honestly. My boss, David, pulled me into his office—which, by the way, he just had redecorated. He spent like twenty thousand dollars on this ridiculous mid-century modern furniture that isn't even comfortable. You sit in these chairs and you feel like you're sliding onto the floor the whole time. And he had this massive abstract painting put up behind his desk. It looks like someone just threw buckets of gray and mustard yellow paint at a canvas. He told me it was an "investment piece," but I know for a fact the gallery he bought it from just inflates the prices to make corporate buyers feel sophisticated. We actually looked at the art market as an alternative asset class a few years ago, and the liquidity is just terrible. You buy this stuff thinking it's going to appreciate, but the secondary market is entirely dependent on trends and gallery representation. It's basically a shell game for rich people to park their money. 

But anyway, yeah, the warning. And it's not just work. I used to do this volunteer teaching thing on weekends, and yoga, and I had a book club... but I've just stopped showing up to all of it. I don't have the energy. The book club was getting annoying anyway, to be fair. Last month they picked this memoir by some actress who thinks she's a wellness guru now, and... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, distress and affecting my life. Yeah, I guess you could say my life outside of work is basically gone now.

Well, yeah, I guess I have. I just... I don't really care about them anymore. The yoga, especially. I mean, I used to go three times a week, but the studio I was going to got bought out by this big corporate chain. 

It's actually fascinating from a business perspective, because you see these boutique fitness places get acquired, and the parent company immediately tries to standardize everything to scale it up. They changed all the instructors' schedules, brought in these cheaper, branded mats that smell like chemicals, and started pushing these ridiculous monthly subscription tiers. It's the classic private equity playbook—slash operational costs, squeeze the existing customer base, and try to package it for an IPO or a secondary buyout within five years. We looked at a similar roll-up strategy in the boutique coffee space last year, and the problem is you destroy the brand equity. The customers are paying a premium for the community and the specialized experience, so when you commoditize it, they churn. And their customer acquisition costs are going through the roof right now because digital ad spend is so inefficient since the new privacy updates. 

Anyway, the yoga studio is just... it's not the same. And the volunteer teaching, I just... I don't know, I couldn't get out of bed for it. What was the question again? Oh, losing interest. Yeah, I just... I don't do any of it anymore. It's all just too much effort.

I usually just sit on the couch.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long it usually takes you to fall asleep, and how often you wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I tried pointing this out in the committee meeting, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, falling asleep. Yeah, it takes hours, and then I'm up at 3 AM. It's just not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you wake up feeling unrefreshed, like you haven't really slept at all, even if you were asleep for part of the night?\n\nPatient: Unrefreshed? I have this German sedan, and the battery is supposed to last for five years, but it died after three. But they wouldn't honor the warranty. The service manager, this guy named Chad with way too much hair gel, just kept pointing at the fine print on his little tablet. Yeah, I guess... it's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time. Most days I move through the office and even my apartment with it. It's like I'm just going through the motions, trying not to cry when someone asks me a normal question.

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It's... it's actually been a huge problem. Spreadsheets that used to be second nature to me now take me hours. I just lose the thread of what I'm doing, and I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over again. And even then, I still worry I've missed something obvious before I have to do a presentation. 

Presentations are the worst part right now, honestly. We use this software that our IT department forced on us last quarter, and the interface is an absolute disaster. It's supposed to be this integrated, cloud-based collaborative tool, but the latency is terrible. If two people are in the deck at the same time, it overwrites changes without tracking versions properly. It's a classic case of enterprise software bloat—they sell the C-suite on this massive feature set, but the actual user experience is so clunky that productivity tanks. We did an analysis on enterprise SaaS companies a while back, and the churn rate for these platforms is actually really high once the initial multi-year contract expires, because the end-users rebel. The switching costs are huge, obviously, which is what the sales reps bank on, but eventually, the inefficiency gets too expensive to ignore. I mean, my VP spent half a meeting yesterday just trying to figure out how to share his screen because the permissions are so convoluted. 

But... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus. I stare at the numbers and they just don't make sense anymore.

I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble staying awake or find it hard to concentrate during the day because of your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Well, staying awake isn't really the issue, but concentrating... yeah. I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over, and I still worry I've missed something obvious before a presentation. \n\nIt's actually a lot like what happened with my dry cleaner last week. I've been going to the same place for six years, right? And I dropped off this silk blouse—the navy one I usually wear for quarterly reviews. When I went to pick it up, they had completely lost it. But their new computer system—which looks like it was built in 1998, by the way—didn't have it logged properly. \n\nIt's exactly the kind of operational inefficiency we look for when we're doing a teardown of a struggling retail chain. You see these legacy systems, and the management refuses to upgrade because of the upfront capital expenditure, but they bleed money in lost inventory and customer churn. I was looking at a regional department store's Q3 numbers just last month, and their shrinkage was up 4% year-over-year entirely because of outdated point-of-sale software. It's just... it's basic infrastructure. If you can't track a navy silk blouse, how are you tracking your quarterly revenue? \n\nBut anyway... sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus on the numbers anymore. You sit in these chairs and you feel like you're sliding onto the floor the whole time. And he had this massive abstract painting put up behind his desk. It looks like someone just threw buckets of gray and mustard yellow paint at a canvas. He told me it was an \"investment piece,\" but I know for a fact the gallery he bought it from just inflates the prices to make corporate buyers feel sophisticated. We actually looked at the art market as an alternative asset class a few years ago, and the liquidity is just terrible. You buy this stuff thinking it's going to appreciate, but the secondary market is entirely dependent on trends and gallery representation. It's basically a shell game for rich people to park their money. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the warning. And it's not just work. I used to do this volunteer teaching thing on weekends, and yoga, and I had a book club... but I've just stopped showing up to all of it. I don't have the energy. I don't know... I guess so? It's hard to remember what it was like before, honestly. Everything just feels like it's been this way forever. Even the book club, I just... I stopped reading the books. It just felt pointless. 

And book publishing is such a bizarre industry right now anyway. I mean, the margins are incredibly thin, and they're so reliant on these massive blockbuster hits to subsidize the rest of the list. It's basically a venture capital model, but with way less upside. They throw all this advance money at celebrity memoirs or established authors, and the mid-list writers just get completely ignored. We were looking at a potential acquisition of a mid-sized publisher a few years ago, and their backlist was the only thing generating consistent cash flow. The frontlist was just a gamble every single season. And now with self-publishing platforms and audiobooks taking up so much market share, the traditional distribution channels are getting squeezed. The brick-and-mortar bookstores that are left are basically just surviving on selling tote bags and greeting cards because the unit economics on physical books are so terrible when you factor in returns. They have this archaic system where bookstores can just return unsold inventory for full credit, which makes revenue recognition an absolute nightmare for the publishers. 

But... wait, what was the question? Before the yoga studio? Yeah, I don't know. I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. None of it sounds fun.

We're financial analysts, not a tech startup in 2012. It just feels so forced and hollow. I had to sit in one once when the regular chairs were taken, and it took me forever to get out of it. My skirt got completely wrinkled, and I had to go into a meeting with the VP looking like an absolute mess. I don't know why Greg still has a job, to be honest. \n\nBut... um, what were we talking about? Oh, fatigue. Yeah, no, I don't really notice being excessively tired during the day. I just keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or like you can’t relax, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Physically restless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really fidget or pace around or anything. I actually sit pretty still. \n\nMy sister did tell me I should try going to one of those sound baths to \"relax\" my body, though, so I went to this place downtown last weekend. It was ridiculously overpriced, first of all. They charged seventy dollars for forty-five minutes of some guy hitting brass bowls with a mallet. And the bowls weren't even polished. I mean, if you're going to charge a premium rate, your equipment should reflect that, right? It's just basic business sense. \n\nIt actually reminded me of when I have to look at the expense reports for these mid-cap tech firms and they have all these phantom write-offs for \"wellness retreats\" that are really just luxury vacations in Tulum. I saw one last year where they wrote off a four-thousand-dollar dinner as team building. Four thousand dollars! The IRS would have an absolute field day if they actually looked at those itemized receipts. I mean, how many margaritas can a team of six even drink? It's blatant fraud, but nobody seems to care anymore. Everyone just rubber-stamps everything. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, being restless. No, I don't really have that issue. I usually just sit on the couch.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long it usually takes you to fall asleep, and how often you wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I'll just lie awake replaying client models in my head for hours. And then I'll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach completely tight, and I just end up going to work feeling like I never actually rested at all. \n\nIt's actually really frustrating because I bought this new mattress last month. It was supposed to be one of those hybrid ones with the cooling gel layer that all the reviews said would change your life. I spent three thousand dollars on it, and the delivery guys scuffed my hardwood floor in the hallway when they brought it in. I tried calling the company's customer service to complain about the damage, but I just got stuck in this endless phone tree. \"Press 1 for sales, press 2 for returns.\" I swear, companies don't want you to actually talk to a human being anymore. They just want you to give up. \n\nIt's like this one telecom company we were analyzing last quarter. They completely restructured their customer support division to rely almost entirely on AI chatbots, and their stock went up 12% just on the announcement. But their actual customer retention numbers are terrible. Nobody looks at the long-term fundamentals anymore, they just look at the short-term cost savings. It's so short-sighted. I tried pointing this out in the committee meeting, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, falling asleep. Yeah, it takes hours, and then I'm up at 3 AM. It's just not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you wake up feeling unrefreshed, like you haven't really slept at all, even if you were asleep for part of the night?\n\nPatient: Unrefreshed? Yeah, I mean, like I said, I feel like I never actually rested. Even if I get a couple of hours, I wake up feeling completely hollowed out. It's like my brain is still running the numbers while my body is just... there. \n\nIt reminds me of my car battery, actually. I have this German sedan, and the battery is supposed to last for five years, but it died after three. I took it to the dealership, and they tried to tell me it was because I don't drive it enough on the highway to charge the alternator. I drive twenty miles each way to the office! But they wouldn't honor the warranty. The service manager, this guy named Chad with way too much hair gel, just kept pointing at the fine print on his little tablet. It's ridiculous how these luxury brands treat you once they have your money. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on the waiting room there. They had this espresso machine—not like the one at work, but still annoying—and the TV was blasting some morning talk show where they were discussing \"superfoods.\" They spent ten minutes talking about chia seeds. Chia seeds! They're just seeds! People will buy anything if you put the word \"super\" in front of it. We saw a whole portfolio of these health-food startups tank last year because their entire business model was based on Instagram influencers pretending to like kale powder. It's all just marketing smoke and mirrors. \n\nBut... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, waking up unrefreshed. Yeah, definitely. I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble staying awake or find it hard to concentrate during the day because of your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Well, staying awake isn't really the issue, but concentrating... yeah. It's getting bad. Spreadsheets that used to be second nature to me now take hours. I just... I lose the thread. I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over, and I still worry I've missed something obvious before a presentation. \n\nIt's actually a lot like what happened with my dry cleaner last week. I've been going to the same place for six years, right? And I dropped off this silk blouse—the navy one I usually wear for quarterly reviews. When I went to pick it up, they had completely lost it. The owner, Mr. Kim, spent twenty minutes looking through the racks, and then he tried to tell me I never brought it in. I showed him the ticket! It had the itemized number right there. But their new computer system—which looks like it was built in 1998, by the way—didn't have it logged properly. \n\nIt's exactly the kind of operational inefficiency we look for when we're doing a teardown of a struggling retail chain. You see these legacy systems, and the management refuses to upgrade because of the upfront capital expenditure, but they bleed money in lost inventory and customer churn. I was looking at a regional department store's Q3 numbers just last month, and their shrinkage was up 4% year-over-year entirely because of outdated point-of-sale software. It's just... it's basic infrastructure. If you can't track a navy silk blouse, how are you tracking your quarterly revenue? \n\nBut anyway... sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus on the numbers anymore. It takes me forever to get through a simple model.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel irritable, frustrated, or on edge during the day because of your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Well, I don't know if it's the sleep, but yeah, I guess I'm pretty on edge lately. Everything just feels like it's grating on me. \n\nLike the other day at the grocery store. I just wanted to get some of those honeycrisp apples, right? And the produce guy was blocking the entire aisle with his pallet jack, just standing there texting on his phone. I asked him nicely if I could squeeze by, and he gave me this look, like I was the one inconveniencing him. It's just... the lack of basic customer service everywhere is astounding. \n\nIt's a huge problem in the service sector right now. We were looking at labor productivity metrics for a major supermarket chain, and their turnover rate is so high they can't even maintain basic store standards. They're spending millions on these automated checkout kiosks to reduce headcount, but the machines are always breaking down. You go in, and there's a line of fifteen people waiting for one poor teenager to come clear an \"unexpected item in bagging area\" error. It's a complete misallocation of resources. If they just paid a competitive wage and trained their staff, their throughput would be so much better. I mean, the margins in grocery are razor-thin to begin with, you can't afford that kind of friction at the point of sale. I was trying to explain this to the junior analyst on my team, but he just kept staring at me like... \n\nWait, sorry. What was the question? Irritable? I mean, I guess I haven't really noticed if it's from the sleep, but... yeah, maybe. I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep difficulty caused significant distress or started affecting your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's definitely affecting work. I mean, I actually got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations just keep slipping. I keep missing things. \n\nIt's so embarrassing, honestly. My boss, David, pulled me into his office—which, by the way, he just had redecorated. He spent like twenty thousand dollars on this ridiculous mid-century modern furniture that isn't even comfortable. You sit in these chairs and you feel like you're sliding onto the floor the whole time. And he had this massive abstract painting put up behind his desk. It looks like someone just threw buckets of gray and mustard yellow paint at a canvas. He told me it was an \"investment piece,\" but I know for a fact the gallery he bought it from just inflates the prices to make corporate buyers feel sophisticated. We actually looked at the art market as an alternative asset class a few years ago, and the liquidity is just terrible. You buy this stuff thinking it's going to appreciate, but the secondary market is entirely dependent on trends and gallery representation. It's basically a shell game for rich people to park their money. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the warning. And it's not just work. I used to do this volunteer teaching thing on weekends, and yoga, and I had a book club... but I've just stopped showing up to all of it. I don't have the energy. The book club was getting annoying anyway, to be fair. Last month they picked this memoir by some actress who thinks she's a wellness guru now, and... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, distress and affecting my life. Yeah, I guess you could say my life outside of work is basically gone now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer teaching, yoga, or book club?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. I just... I don't really care about them anymore. The yoga, especially. I mean, I used to go three times a week, but the studio I was going to got bought out by this big corporate chain. \n\nIt's actually fascinating from a business perspective, because you see these boutique fitness places get acquired, and the parent company immediately tries to standardize everything to scale it up. They changed all the instructors' schedules, brought in these cheaper, branded mats that smell like chemicals, and started pushing these ridiculous monthly subscription tiers. It's the classic private equity playbook—slash operational costs, squeeze the existing customer base, and try to package it for an IPO or a secondary buyout within five years. We looked at a similar roll-up strategy in the boutique coffee space last year, and the problem is you destroy the brand equity. The customers are paying a premium for the community and the specialized experience, so when you commoditize it, they churn. And their customer acquisition costs are going through the roof right now because digital ad spend is so inefficient since the new privacy updates. \n\nAnyway, the yoga studio is just... it's not the same. And the volunteer teaching, I just... I don't know, I couldn't get out of bed for it. What was the question again? Oh, losing interest. Yeah, I just... I don't do any of it anymore. It's all just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess... it's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time. Most days I move through the office and even my apartment with it. It's like I'm just going through the motions, trying not to cry when someone asks me a normal question. \n\nLike the other day, the barista at the coffee shop in my building just asked if I wanted room for milk, and I almost burst into tears. And it's a ridiculous place anyway. They source their beans from this collective in Colombia, which sounds great for ESG reporting, but their supply chain logistics are a nightmare. They're constantly running out of inventory because they rely on this single boutique distributor that doesn't have the warehouse capacity to buffer against shipping delays. We actually modeled out the risk for a similar single-source supply chain in the specialty foods sector, and the variance in their quarterly earnings was wild. You can't run a retail operation when your core product is out of stock three days a month. You lose the habitual customer. The lifetime value of a daily coffee drinker is massive, and they're just throwing it away because they won't diversify their supplier base. I tried to hint at this to the manager once, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Feeling down? Yeah. Yeah, it's just... it's heavy. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or concentrate on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's... it's actually been a huge problem. Spreadsheets that used to be second nature to me now take me hours. I just lose the thread of what I'm doing, and I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over again. And even then, I still worry I've missed something obvious before I have to do a presentation. \n\nPresentations are the worst part right now, honestly. We use this software that our IT department forced on us last quarter, and the interface is an absolute disaster. It's supposed to be this integrated, cloud-based collaborative tool, but the latency is terrible. If two people are in the deck at the same time, it overwrites changes without tracking versions properly. It's a classic case of enterprise software bloat—they sell the C-suite on this massive feature set, but the actual user experience is so clunky that productivity tanks. We did an analysis on enterprise SaaS companies a while back, and the churn rate for these platforms is actually really high once the initial multi-year contract expires, because the end-users rebel. The switching costs are huge, obviously, which is what the sales reps bank on, but eventually, the inefficiency gets too expensive to ignore. I mean, my VP spent half a meeting yesterday just trying to figure out how to share his screen because the permissions are so convoluted. \n\nBut... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus. I stare at the numbers and they just don't make sense anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, even before the yoga studio changed ownership or other external changes occurred?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess so? It's hard to remember what it was like before, honestly. Everything just feels like it's been this way forever. Even the book club, I just... I stopped reading the books. It just felt pointless. \n\nAnd book publishing is such a bizarre industry right now anyway. I mean, the margins are incredibly thin, and they're so reliant on these massive blockbuster hits to subsidize the rest of the list. It's basically a venture capital model, but with way less upside. They throw all this advance money at celebrity memoirs or established authors, and the mid-list writers just get completely ignored. We were looking at a potential acquisition of a mid-sized publisher a few years ago, and their backlist was the only thing generating consistent cash flow. The frontlist was just a gamble every single season. And now with self-publishing platforms and audiobooks taking up so much market share, the traditional distribution channels are getting squeezed. The brick-and-mortar bookstores that are left are basically just surviving on selling tote bags and greeting cards because the unit economics on physical books are so terrible when you factor in returns. They have this archaic system where bookstores can just return unsold inventory for full credit, which makes revenue recognition an absolute nightmare for the publishers. \n\nBut... wait, what was the question? Before the yoga studio? Yeah, I don't know. I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. None of it sounds fun.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I guess... yeah, I don't really get anything out of them anymore. The volunteer teaching, especially. I used to love helping those kids with their financial literacy classes, but now it's just exhausting. I mean, the curriculum they use is so outdated anyway. They're still teaching these high schoolers about balancing a checkbook, which is completely irrelevant. Nobody writes checks anymore. 

The entire consumer banking sector has pivoted to digital-first or mobile-only platforms. I mean, look at the neo-banks—they've completely disrupted the traditional fee structures. We were analyzing a regional bank last month, and their tech debt is astronomical. They can't roll out new features without risking a massive outage, so they just keep acquiring smaller fintech startups to bolt onto their existing infrastructure. But the integration costs always end up destroying the ROI of the acquisition. It's a terrible capital allocation strategy, but the board just rubber-stamps it because they're terrified of losing market share to younger demographics. And don't even get me started on how they handle overdraft fees in this regulatory environment—it's a massive compliance risk just waiting to happen. 

Sorry... what were we talking about? Pleasure? Yeah. No, I don't get any pleasure from the teaching. Or the yoga. Or anything, really. It's just all so gray.

I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over, and I still worry I've missed something obvious before a presentation. \n\nIt's actually a lot like what happened with my dry cleaner last week. I've been going to the same place for six years, right? And I dropped off this silk blouse—the navy one I usually wear for quarterly reviews. When I went to pick it up, they had completely lost it. The owner, Mr. Kim, spent twenty minutes looking through the racks, and then he tried to tell me I never brought it in. I showed him the ticket! It had the itemized number right there. But their new computer system—which looks like it was built in 1998, by the way—didn't have it logged properly. \n\nIt's exactly the kind of operational inefficiency we look for when we're doing a teardown of a struggling retail chain. You see these legacy systems, and the management refuses to upgrade because of the upfront capital expenditure, but they bleed money in lost inventory and customer churn. I was looking at a regional department store's Q3 numbers just last month, and their shrinkage was up 4% year-over-year entirely because of outdated point-of-sale software. It's just... it's basic infrastructure. If you can't track a navy silk blouse, how are you tracking your quarterly revenue? \n\nBut anyway... sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus on the numbers anymore. It takes me forever to get through a simple model.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel irritable, frustrated, or on edge during the day because of your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Well, I don't know if it's the sleep, but yeah, I guess I'm pretty on edge lately. Everything just feels like it's grating on me. \n\nLike the other day at the grocery store. I just wanted to get some of those honeycrisp apples, right? And the produce guy was blocking the entire aisle with his pallet jack, just standing there texting on his phone. I asked him nicely if I could squeeze by, and he gave me this look, like I was the one inconveniencing him. It's just... the lack of basic customer service everywhere is astounding. \n\nIt's a huge problem in the service sector right now. We were looking at labor productivity metrics for a major supermarket chain, and their turnover rate is so high they can't even maintain basic store standards. They're spending millions on these automated checkout kiosks to reduce headcount, but the machines are always breaking down. You go in, and there's a line of fifteen people waiting for one poor teenager to come clear an \"unexpected item in bagging area\" error. It's a complete misallocation of resources. If they just paid a competitive wage and trained their staff, their throughput would be so much better. I mean, the margins in grocery are razor-thin to begin with, you can't afford that kind of friction at the point of sale. I was trying to explain this to the junior analyst on my team, but he just kept staring at me like... \n\nWait, sorry. What was the question? Irritable? I mean, I guess I haven't really noticed if it's from the sleep, but... yeah, maybe. I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep difficulty caused significant distress or started affecting your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's definitely affecting work. I mean, I actually got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations just keep slipping. I keep missing things. \n\nIt's so embarrassing, honestly. My boss, David, pulled me into his office—which, by the way, he just had redecorated. He spent like twenty thousand dollars on this ridiculous mid-century modern furniture that isn't even comfortable. You sit in these chairs and you feel like you're sliding onto the floor the whole time. And he had this massive abstract painting put up behind his desk. It looks like someone just threw buckets of gray and mustard yellow paint at a canvas. He told me it was an \"investment piece,\" but I know for a fact the gallery he bought it from just inflates the prices to make corporate buyers feel sophisticated. We actually looked at the art market as an alternative asset class a few years ago, and the liquidity is just terrible. You buy this stuff thinking it's going to appreciate, but the secondary market is entirely dependent on trends and gallery representation. It's basically a shell game for rich people to park their money. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the warning. And it's not just work. I used to do this volunteer teaching thing on weekends, and yoga, and I had a book club... but I've just stopped showing up to all of it. I don't have the energy. The book club was getting annoying anyway, to be fair. Last month they picked this memoir by some actress who thinks she's a wellness guru now, and... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, distress and affecting my life. Yeah, I guess you could say my life outside of work is basically gone now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer teaching, yoga, or book club?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. I just... I don't really care about them anymore. The yoga, especially. I mean, I used to go three times a week, but the studio I was going to got bought out by this big corporate chain. \n\nIt's actually fascinating from a business perspective, because you see these boutique fitness places get acquired, and the parent company immediately tries to standardize everything to scale it up. They changed all the instructors' schedules, brought in these cheaper, branded mats that smell like chemicals, and started pushing these ridiculous monthly subscription tiers. It's the classic private equity playbook—slash operational costs, squeeze the existing customer base, and try to package it for an IPO or a secondary buyout within five years. We looked at a similar roll-up strategy in the boutique coffee space last year, and the problem is you destroy the brand equity. The customers are paying a premium for the community and the specialized experience, so when you commoditize it, they churn. And their customer acquisition costs are going through the roof right now because digital ad spend is so inefficient since the new privacy updates. \n\nAnyway, the yoga studio is just... it's not the same. And the volunteer teaching, I just... I don't know, I couldn't get out of bed for it. What was the question again? Oh, losing interest. Yeah, I just... I don't do any of it anymore. It's all just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess... it's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time. Most days I move through the office and even my apartment with it. It's like I'm just going through the motions, trying not to cry when someone asks me a normal question. \n\nLike the other day, the barista at the coffee shop in my building just asked if I wanted room for milk, and I almost burst into tears. And it's a ridiculous place anyway. They source their beans from this collective in Colombia, which sounds great for ESG reporting, but their supply chain logistics are a nightmare. They're constantly running out of inventory because they rely on this single boutique distributor that doesn't have the warehouse capacity to buffer against shipping delays. We actually modeled out the risk for a similar single-source supply chain in the specialty foods sector, and the variance in their quarterly earnings was wild. You can't run a retail operation when your core product is out of stock three days a month. You lose the habitual customer. The lifetime value of a daily coffee drinker is massive, and they're just throwing it away because they won't diversify their supplier base. I tried to hint at this to the manager once, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Feeling down? Yeah. Yeah, it's just... it's heavy. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or concentrate on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's... it's actually been a huge problem. Spreadsheets that used to be second nature to me now take me hours. I just lose the thread of what I'm doing, and I end up rereading the same assumptions over and over again. And even then, I still worry I've missed something obvious before I have to do a presentation. \n\nPresentations are the worst part right now, honestly. We use this software that our IT department forced on us last quarter, and the interface is an absolute disaster. It's supposed to be this integrated, cloud-based collaborative tool, but the latency is terrible. If two people are in the deck at the same time, it overwrites changes without tracking versions properly. It's a classic case of enterprise software bloat—they sell the C-suite on this massive feature set, but the actual user experience is so clunky that productivity tanks. We did an analysis on enterprise SaaS companies a while back, and the churn rate for these platforms is actually really high once the initial multi-year contract expires, because the end-users rebel. The switching costs are huge, obviously, which is what the sales reps bank on, but eventually, the inefficiency gets too expensive to ignore. I mean, my VP spent half a meeting yesterday just trying to figure out how to share his screen because the permissions are so convoluted. \n\nBut... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus. I stare at the numbers and they just don't make sense anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, even before the yoga studio changed ownership or other external changes occurred?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess so? It's hard to remember what it was like before, honestly. Everything just feels like it's been this way forever. Even the book club, I just... I stopped reading the books. It just felt pointless. \n\nAnd book publishing is such a bizarre industry right now anyway. I mean, the margins are incredibly thin, and they're so reliant on these massive blockbuster hits to subsidize the rest of the list. It's basically a venture capital model, but with way less upside. They throw all this advance money at celebrity memoirs or established authors, and the mid-list writers just get completely ignored. We were looking at a potential acquisition of a mid-sized publisher a few years ago, and their backlist was the only thing generating consistent cash flow. The frontlist was just a gamble every single season. And now with self-publishing platforms and audiobooks taking up so much market share, the traditional distribution channels are getting squeezed. The brick-and-mortar bookstores that are left are basically just surviving on selling tote bags and greeting cards because the unit economics on physical books are so terrible when you factor in returns. They have this archaic system where bookstores can just return unsold inventory for full credit, which makes revenue recognition an absolute nightmare for the publishers. \n\nBut... wait, what was the question? Before the yoga studio? Yeah, I don't know. I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. None of it sounds fun.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer teaching, yoga, or reading?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, I don't really get anything out of them anymore. The volunteer teaching, especially. I used to love helping those kids with their financial literacy classes, but now it's just exhausting. I mean, the curriculum they use is so outdated anyway. They're still teaching these high schoolers about balancing a checkbook, which is completely irrelevant. Nobody writes checks anymore. \n\nThe entire consumer banking sector has pivoted to digital-first or mobile-only platforms. I mean, look at the neo-banks—they've completely disrupted the traditional fee structures. We did an analysis on enterprise SaaS companies a while back, and the churn rate for these platforms is actually really high once the initial multi-year contract expires, because the end-users rebel. The switching costs are huge, obviously, which is what the sales reps bank on, but eventually, the inefficiency gets too expensive to ignore. I mean, my VP spent half a meeting yesterday just trying to figure out how to share his screen because the permissions are so convoluted. \n\nBut... sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't focus. I stare at the numbers and they just don't make sense anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, even before the yoga studio changed ownership or other external changes occurred?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess so? It's hard to remember what it was like before, honestly. Everything just feels like it's been this way forever. Even the book club, I just... I stopped reading the books. It just felt pointless. \n\nAnd book publishing is such a bizarre industry right now anyway. I mean, the margins are incredibly thin, and they're so reliant on these massive blockbuster hits to subsidize the rest of the list. It's basically a venture capital model, but with way less upside. They throw all this advance money at celebrity memoirs or established authors, and the mid-list writers just get completely ignored. We were looking at a potential acquisition of a mid-sized publisher a few years ago, and their backlist was the only thing generating consistent cash flow. The frontlist was just a gamble every single season. And now with self-publishing platforms and audiobooks taking up so much market share, the traditional distribution channels are getting squeezed. The brick-and-mortar bookstores that are left are basically just surviving on selling tote bags and greeting cards because the unit economics on physical books are so terrible when you factor in returns. They have this archaic system where bookstores can just return unsold inventory for full credit, which makes revenue recognition an absolute nightmare for the publishers. \n\nBut... wait, what was the question? Before the yoga studio? Yeah, I don't know. I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. None of it sounds fun.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer teaching, yoga, or reading?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, I don't really get anything out of them anymore. The volunteer teaching, especially. I used to love helping those kids with their financial literacy classes, but now it's just exhausting. I mean, the curriculum they use is so outdated anyway. They're still teaching these high schoolers about balancing a checkbook, which is completely irrelevant. Nobody writes checks anymore. \n\nThe entire consumer banking sector has pivoted to digital-first or mobile-only platforms. I mean, look at the neo-banks—they've completely disrupted the traditional fee structures. The legacy banks are scrambling to catch up, but their legacy IT systems are basically held together with duct tape and COBOL code from the 1980s. We were analyzing a regional bank last month, and their tech debt is astronomical. They can't roll out new features without risking a massive outage, so they just keep acquiring smaller fintech startups to bolt onto their existing infrastructure. But the integration costs always end up destroying the ROI of the acquisition. It's a terrible capital allocation strategy, but the board just rubber-stamps it because they're terrified of losing market share to younger demographics. And don't even get me started on how they handle overdraft fees in this regulatory environment—it's a massive compliance risk just waiting to happen. \n\nSorry... what were we talking about? Pleasure? Yeah. No, I don't get any pleasure from the teaching. Or the yoga. Or anything, really. It's just all so gray."
